People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess I’m here because... I just don’t care about anything anymore. It’s like all the things I used to do, I just can't be bothered. Not my art classes, or board-game nights, or hiking... I was even supposed to be getting back to the soccer team, but... I don't know. Now I just sit in my room while everything just kind of slips away. 

Which is a shame because I used to really love those game nights. My friend Jessica used to host them on Thursdays, and she always made this amazing cheese dip in this tiny little slow cooker she found at a garage sale. It had these painted orange daisies on the side of it. She collects a lot of vintage kitchen stuff, actually, her whole apartment looks like it's stuck in the 1970s. She even has one of those rotary phones on the wall, though I don't think it actually works. I keep meaning to ask her where she finds all that stuff, but... yeah. I just stay in my room now.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, there's no pleasure. Like I said, I just don't care about anything anymore. It's not just that I don't feel like doing them, it's that when I try, or even think about trying, it's all just... flat. The art classes, the game nights, the hiking... even the soccer team. I just sit in my room while everything slips away. 

I used to have my art supplies set up by the window in my room. It's a nice window, actually, it gets the morning sun. But the glass has this tiny crack in the bottom corner. The landlord said a bird flew into it before I moved in. I always wondered what kind of bird it was. Maybe a robin? We used to have robins in our yard when I was little. They'd pull worms right out of the grass after it rained. The smell of rain on pavement always reminds me of that house. We had a driveway that was so steep, my dad's car used to scrape the bottom every time he pulled in... but yeah. No pleasure. I just sit in my room.

It's been a long time. I think it became constant around the following summer, so... maybe a year and four months now? That summer was really hot. I remember the air conditioner in my apartment broke right in the middle of July. The repair guy took three days to come out, and he was this older man who kept complaining about his knees. He said he needed surgery but couldn't afford the time off work. He had this big toolbox that was covered in stickers from different national parks. I've never been to a national park, actually. I always wanted to go to Yosemite. I saw a documentary about it once on public television. The narrator had this incredibly soothing voice, kind of like Morgan Freeman, but not him. I think I fell asleep watching it, which is weird because I usually have so much trouble sleeping. But yeah, about a year and four months of just... feeling like this.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it really depends on the day. Sometimes making coffee feels like I'm lifting wet cement. Every movement is just... dragging. The coffee maker I have is this old drip machine I bought at a garage sale. It's an awful shade of yellow, like mustard that's been left out in the sun too long. The lady who sold it to me had these huge plastic earrings shaped like parrots. They clacked against her jaw when she talked. She told me she was moving to Florida to live with her sister who breeds miniature poodles. I always thought poodles were kind of high-maintenance dogs, with all the grooming they need. My neighbor used to have a standard poodle that would bark at the mailman every single day right at 11:00 AM. Our mailman was this really tall guy who always wore shorts, even in December when it was snowing... but anyway. Yeah, sometimes I'm just so slow. But then other days, I just pace. I'll walk from the kitchen to the window over and over until my legs actually ache.

Yeah, I've lost weight. My jeans have gotten so loose lately that I actually have to knot the waistband just to keep them up. I just... I go most of the day without eating because food just tastes like cardboard to me now. Even my favorite foods. I used to make this really good baked ziti. My grandmother taught me how to make the sauce from scratch. She had this enormous cast-iron pot that she brought over from Italy, and it weighed an absolute ton. I don't know how she lifted it, honestly, because she was only about four foot eleven. She used to wear these thick woolen sweaters, even in the summer, because she said her blood was thin. She kept peppermint candies in the pockets, the kind with the red and white swirls wrapped in that clear cellophane that crinkles really loudly. I tried to find those exact candies at the grocery store last week, but the aisle was being restocked by this teenager who was listening to music so loud I could hear the bass through his headphones. It was some kind of electronic dance music, I think. But anyway, yeah, I don't really eat much anymore.

We had these bright blue jerseys that always got grass stains on the elbows no matter how much you bleached them. I tried soaking mine in this special eco-friendly detergent once, but it just made it smell like pine needles. My mom used to use pine cleaner on our kitchen floors when I was a kid. She'd mop every Saturday morning while listening to ABBA on this old cassette player. The tape deck would always jam on \"Dancing Queen,\" and she'd have to pull the tape out with a pencil... but no, I don't enjoy anything. I just sit in my room and watch it all slip away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure at all when you do manage to engage in these activities?\n\nPatient: No, there's no pleasure. Like I said, I just don't care about anything anymore. It's not just that I don't feel like doing them, it's that when I try, or even think about trying, it's all just... flat. The art classes, the game nights, the hiking... even the soccer team. I just sit in my room while everything slips away. \n\nI used to have my art supplies set up by the window in my room. It's a nice window, actually, it gets the morning sun. But the glass has this tiny crack in the bottom corner. The landlord said a bird flew into it before I moved in. I always wondered what kind of bird it was. Maybe a robin? We used to have robins in our yard when I was little. They'd pull worms right out of the grass after it rained. The smell of rain on pavement always reminds me of that house. We had a driveway that was so steep, my dad's car used to scrape the bottom every time he pulled in... but yeah. No pleasure. I just sit in my room.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It's been a long time. I think it became constant around the following summer, so... maybe a year and four months now? That summer was really hot. I remember the air conditioner in my apartment broke right in the middle of July. The repair guy took three days to come out, and he was this older man who kept complaining about his knees. He said he needed surgery but couldn't afford the time off work. He had this big toolbox that was covered in stickers from different national parks. I've never been to a national park, actually. I always wanted to go to Yosemite. I saw a documentary about it once on public television. The narrator had this incredibly soothing voice, kind of like Morgan Freeman, but not him. I think I fell asleep watching it, which is weird because I usually have so much trouble sleeping. But yeah, about a year and four months of just... feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Well, it really depends on the day. Sometimes making coffee feels like I'm lifting wet cement. Every movement is just... dragging. The coffee maker I have is this old drip machine I bought at a garage sale. It's an awful shade of yellow, like mustard that's been left out in the sun too long. The lady who sold it to me had these huge plastic earrings shaped like parrots. They clacked against her jaw when she talked. She told me she was moving to Florida to live with her sister who breeds miniature poodles. I always thought poodles were kind of high-maintenance dogs, with all the grooming they need. My neighbor used to have a standard poodle that would bark at the mailman every single day right at 11:00 AM. Our mailman was this really tall guy who always wore shorts, even in December when it was snowing... but anyway. Yeah, sometimes I'm just so slow. But then other days, I just pace. I'll walk from the kitchen to the window over and over until my legs actually ache.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I've lost weight. My jeans have gotten so loose lately that I actually have to knot the waistband just to keep them up. I just... I go most of the day without eating because food just tastes like cardboard to me now. Even my favorite foods. I used to make this really good baked ziti. My grandmother taught me how to make the sauce from scratch. She had this enormous cast-iron pot that she brought over from Italy, and it weighed an absolute ton. I don't know how she lifted it, honestly, because she was only about four foot eleven. She used to wear these thick woolen sweaters, even in the summer, because she said her blood was thin. She kept peppermint candies in the pockets, the kind with the red and white swirls wrapped in that clear cellophane that crinkles really loudly. I tried to find those exact candies at the grocery store last week, but the aisle was being restocked by this teenager who was listening to music so loud I could hear the bass through his headphones. Well, I think I mentioned this just a bit ago, but yeah, I do. It's either one extreme or the other, honestly. Some days I'll just pace, like, back and forth from the kitchen to the window, over and over until my legs actually ache. The floorboards by the window squeak a little. He's this guy who always wears these faded polo shirts with the collars popped up, like he's stuck in an 80s movie or something. He drives this bright red convertible that he parks right in front of the building, taking up two spaces. My neighbor, the one who used to have the poodle, left a really angry note on his windshield once. She wrote it in pink glitter pen, which I thought was a funny choice for an angry note. I used to love those glitter pens when I was in middle school. We'd write notes to each other and fold them into little triangles. I had this one friend, Sarah, who could fold them so perfectly they looked like origami. She ended up moving to Seattle in the eighth grade because her dad got a job at Boeing. I always wondered if it rains as much there as people say. But um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the pacing. Yeah... I have. It's just... I keep having these thoughts that I just don't want to wake up anymore. It's like this heavy blanket over everything. My friend actually came over the other day and found me just sitting there, and I guess I had been saying out loud that I couldn't keep living like this. She got really scared. She's known me since we were in college. We took this art history class together, and the professor was this eccentric guy who always wore bow ties. He had a different bow tie for every day of the week, and on Fridays he'd wear one with little cartoon dinosaurs on it. He said his grandson picked it out for him. His grandson was named Oliver, I think, or maybe Leo. It was one of those names that got really popular a few years ago. I remember reading an article about how baby names go in cycles, like how all the vintage names from the 1920s are coming back now. My great-aunt was named Pearl, and she collected ceramic thimbles from all over the world. But yeah, my friend found me and I was just saying I couldn't do it anymore. I just want to go to sleep and not wake up.

She's known me since we were in college. We took this art history class together, and the professor was this eccentric guy who always wore bow ties. He had a different bow tie for every day of the week, and on Fridays he'd wear one with little cartoon dinosaurs on it. He said his grandson picked it out for him. His grandson was named Oliver, I think, or maybe Leo. It was one of those names that got really popular a few years ago. I remember reading an article about how baby names go in cycles, like how all the vintage names from the 1920s are coming back now. My great-aunt was named Pearl, and she collected ceramic thimbles from all over the world. She had a whole display case for them in her hallway, right next to this grandfather clock that chimed so loudly it would make you jump if you weren't expecting it. But yeah, my friend found me and I was just saying I couldn't do it anymore. I just want to go to sleep and not wake up.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually feel like you're moving slowly or is it more that some days you're slow and others you're pacing back and forth?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I think I kind of just said this, but yeah, it really depends on the day. It's like I'm stuck between two extremes. Some days I just pace from the kitchen to the window, over and over again, until my legs literally ache. The linoleum in the kitchen is starting to peel up at the corners, actually. I could never drink them, but my brother loved them. He lives in Denver now, working as a graphic designer. He designed the logo for this local brewery out there, the one with the mountain goat on it. I've never been to Denver, but I hear the altitude can really mess with you if you're not used to it. But anyway, yeah, other times, I move so slowly that just trying to make a cup of coffee feels like I'm trying to lift wet cement. It's just exhausting.

Honestly, it's hard to say which one happens more. I feel like I'm just always bouncing between them. Like I was saying, some days I'm just pacing, kitchen to the window, over and over until my legs are just throbbing. And then other days, I'm so slow, like trying to make coffee is like lifting wet cement. I actually bought this new coffee a few weeks ago, a dark roast from that little shop on the corner. The barista there has this really intricate tattoo of a compass on his forearm. He told me it took three sessions to finish. I've always thought about getting a tattoo, maybe a small bird or something on my ankle, but I'm terrified of needles. When I was a kid, I had to get blood drawn, and I actually passed out right there in the chair. The nurse had to give me a juice box and a sticker, like I was five years old. It was one of those scratch-and-sniff stickers, grape scented. I used to collect those in a binder. I had pages and pages of them. But um, yeah, the pacing and the slowness... they just kind of take turns ruining my week. I don't really keep track of which one wins.

My friend Jessica used to host them on Thursdays, and she always made this amazing cheese dip in this tiny little slow cooker she found at a garage sale. It had these painted orange daisies on the side of it. She collects a lot of vintage kitchen stuff, actually, her whole apartment looks like it's stuck in the 1970s. She even has one of those rotary phones on the wall, though I don't think it actually works. I keep meaning to ask her where she finds all that stuff, but... yeah. I just stay in my room now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah... most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. It just sits there. It feels like there’s no future for me beyond the apartment walls. I stare at those walls a lot, actually. They have this weird textured wallpaper that looks kind of like oatmeal. I tried to peel a little piece off in the corner by the baseboard once, but it’s glued on there really tight. The landlord said it was installed in the nineties by the previous owners, who apparently had a bunch of cats. I’m actually allergic to cats, which is why I never got one, even though Jessica keeps telling me a pet would be good for me. She has a pug named Barnaby who snores louder than a person. He has this little plaid sweater he wears in the winter when she takes him for walks... but anyway, yeah. Just that heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to love, even if you don't feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: No, I... I really don't. Like I was saying before, I just don't care about any of it anymore. The art classes, the hiking, the game nights... it's all just... nothing to me now. I don't even care about the soccer team, which is wild because I played for three years. We had these bright blue jerseys that always got grass stains on the elbows no matter how much you bleached them. I tried soaking mine in this special eco-friendly detergent once, but it just made it smell like pine needles. My mom used to use pine cleaner on our kitchen floors when I was a kid. She'd mop every Saturday morning while listening to ABBA on this old cassette player. The tape deck would always jam on \"Dancing Queen,\" and she'd have to pull the tape out with a pencil... but no, I don't enjoy anything. I just sit in my room and watch it all slip away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure at all when you do manage to engage in these activities?\n\nPatient: No, there's no pleasure. Like I said, I just don't care about anything anymore. It's not just that I don't feel like doing them, it's that when I try, or even think about trying, it's all just... flat. The art classes, the game nights, the hiking... even the soccer team. I just sit in my room while everything slips away. \n\nI used to have my art supplies set up by the window in my room. It's a nice window, actually, it gets the morning sun. But the glass has this tiny crack in the bottom corner. The landlord said a bird flew into it before I moved in. I always wondered what kind of bird it was. Maybe a robin? We used to have robins in our yard when I was little. They'd pull worms right out of the grass after it rained. The smell of rain on pavement always reminds me of that house. We had a driveway that was so steep, my dad's car used to scrape the bottom every time he pulled in... but yeah. No pleasure. I just sit in my room.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It's been a long time. I think it became constant around the following summer, so... maybe a year and four months now? That summer was really hot. I remember the air conditioner in my apartment broke right in the middle of July. The repair guy took three days to come out, and he was this older man who kept complaining about his knees. He said he needed surgery but couldn't afford the time off work. He had this big toolbox that was covered in stickers from different national parks. I've never been to a national park, actually. I always wanted to go to Yosemite. I saw a documentary about it once on public television. The narrator had this incredibly soothing voice, kind of like Morgan Freeman, but not him. I think I fell asleep watching it, which is weird because I usually have so much trouble sleeping. But yeah, about a year and four months of just... feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Well, it really depends on the day. Sometimes making coffee feels like I'm lifting wet cement. Every movement is just... dragging. The coffee maker I have is this old drip machine I bought at a garage sale. It's an awful shade of yellow, like mustard that's been left out in the sun too long. The lady who sold it to me had these huge plastic earrings shaped like parrots. They clacked against her jaw when she talked. She told me she was moving to Florida to live with her sister who breeds miniature poodles. I always thought poodles were kind of high-maintenance dogs, with all the grooming they need. My neighbor used to have a standard poodle that would bark at the mailman every single day right at 11:00 AM. Our mailman was this really tall guy who always wore shorts, even in December when it was snowing... but anyway. Yeah, sometimes I'm just so slow. But then other days, I just pace. I'll walk from the kitchen to the window over and over until my legs actually ache.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I've lost weight. My jeans have gotten so loose lately that I actually have to knot the waistband just to keep them up. I just... I go most of the day without eating because food just tastes like cardboard to me now. Even my favorite foods. I used to make this really good baked ziti. My grandmother taught me how to make the sauce from scratch. She had this enormous cast-iron pot that she brought over from Italy, and it weighed an absolute ton. I don't know how she lifted it, honestly, because she was only about four foot eleven. She used to wear these thick woolen sweaters, even in the summer, because she said her blood was thin. She kept peppermint candies in the pockets, the kind with the red and white swirls wrapped in that clear cellophane that crinkles really loudly. I tried to find those exact candies at the grocery store last week, but the aisle was being restocked by this teenager who was listening to music so loud I could hear the bass through his headphones. It was some kind of electronic dance music, I think. He drives this bright red convertible that he parks right in front of the building, taking up two spaces. I used to love those glitter pens when I was in middle school. We'd write notes to each other and fold them into little triangles. I just want to go to sleep and not wake up.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel like you're moving slowly or is it more that some days you're slow and others you're pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: I think I kind of just said this, but yeah, it really depends on the day. It's like I'm stuck between two extremes. Some days I just pace from the kitchen to the window, over and over again, until my legs literally ache. It's just exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Is the pacing or the slow movement more frequent for you overall?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it's hard to say which one happens more. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I guess I have. I mean, I go most of the day without eating anything at all because everything just tastes like cardboard lately. It's like there's no flavor left in the world. And my jeans have gotten so loose I actually have to knot the waistband to keep them up. I was trying to find a belt the other day, and I dug through my closet and found this old braided leather one I bought at a flea market years ago. It was one of those huge outdoor markets where people sell everything from antique furniture to homemade jam. I bought a jar of strawberry rhubarb jam from this older woman who was wearing a bright purple sun hat. She told me the rhubarb came from her own garden. She had this massive garden, she said, with tomatoes and zucchini and these huge sunflowers that grew taller than her fence. I tried to grow sunflowers once, but the squirrels kept digging up the seeds before they could even sprout. Squirrels are so persistent, you know? They just keep coming back. But yeah, the weight thing... I just don't have any interest in food right now.

I just stay in my room now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah... most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. It just sits there. It feels like there’s no future for me beyond the apartment walls. I stare at those walls a lot, actually. They have this weird textured wallpaper that looks kind of like oatmeal. I tried to peel a little piece off in the corner by the baseboard once, but it’s glued on there really tight. The landlord said it was installed in the nineties by the previous owners, who apparently had a bunch of cats. I tried soaking mine in this special eco-friendly detergent once, but it just made it smell like pine needles. My mom used to use pine cleaner on our kitchen floors when I was a kid. She'd mop every Saturday morning while listening to ABBA on this old cassette player. The tape deck would always jam on \"Dancing Queen,\" and she'd have to pull the tape out with a pencil... but no, I don't enjoy anything. I just sit in my room and watch it all slip away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure at all when you do manage to engage in these activities?\n\nPatient: No, there's no pleasure. Like I said, I just don't care about anything anymore. It's not just that I don't feel like doing them, it's that when I try, or even think about trying, it's all just... flat. The art classes, the game nights, the hiking... even the soccer team. I just sit in my room while everything slips away. \n\nI used to have my art supplies set up by the window in my room. It's a nice window, actually, it gets the morning sun. But the glass has this tiny crack in the bottom corner. The landlord said a bird flew into it before I moved in. I always wondered what kind of bird it was. Maybe a robin? We used to have robins in our yard when I was little. They'd pull worms right out of the grass after it rained. The smell of rain on pavement always reminds me of that house. We had a driveway that was so steep, my dad's car used to scrape the bottom every time he pulled in... but yeah. No pleasure. I just sit in my room.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It's been a long time. I think it became constant around the following summer, so... maybe a year and four months now? That summer was really hot. I remember the air conditioner in my apartment broke right in the middle of July. The repair guy took three days to come out, and he was this older man who kept complaining about his knees. He said he needed surgery but couldn't afford the time off work. He had this big toolbox that was covered in stickers from different national parks. I've never been to a national park, actually. I always wanted to go to Yosemite. I saw a documentary about it once on public television. The narrator had this incredibly soothing voice, kind of like Morgan Freeman, but not him. I think I fell asleep watching it, which is weird because I usually have so much trouble sleeping. But yeah, about a year and four months of just... feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Well, it really depends on the day. Sometimes making coffee feels like I'm lifting wet cement. Every movement is just... dragging. The coffee maker I have is this old drip machine I bought at a garage sale. It's an awful shade of yellow, like mustard that's been left out in the sun too long. The lady who sold it to me had these huge plastic earrings shaped like parrots. They clacked against her jaw when she talked. She told me she was moving to Florida to live with her sister who breeds miniature poodles. I always thought poodles were kind of high-maintenance dogs, with all the grooming they need. My neighbor used to have a standard poodle that would bark at the mailman every single day right at 11:00 AM. Our mailman was this really tall guy who always wore shorts, even in December when it was snowing... but anyway. Yeah, sometimes I'm just so slow. But then other days, I just pace. I'll walk from the kitchen to the window over and over until my legs actually ache.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I've lost weight. My jeans have gotten so loose lately that I actually have to knot the waistband just to keep them up. I just... I go most of the day without eating because food just tastes like cardboard to me now. Even my favorite foods. I used to make this really good baked ziti. My grandmother taught me how to make the sauce from scratch. She had this enormous cast-iron pot that she brought over from Italy, and it weighed an absolute ton. I don't know how she lifted it, honestly, because she was only about four foot eleven. She used to wear these thick woolen sweaters, even in the summer, because she said her blood was thin. She kept peppermint candies in the pockets, the kind with the red and white swirls wrapped in that clear cellophane that crinkles really loudly. I tried to find those exact candies at the grocery store last week, but the aisle was being restocked by this teenager who was listening to music so loud I could hear the bass through his headphones. It was some kind of electronic dance music, I think. But anyway, yeah, I don't really eat much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself pacing back and forth in your room?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I mentioned this just a bit ago, but yeah, I do. He's this guy who always wears these faded polo shirts with the collars popped up, like he's stuck in an 80s movie or something. He drives this bright red convertible that he parks right in front of the building, taking up two spaces. My neighbor, the one who used to have the poodle, left a really angry note on his windshield once. She wrote it in pink glitter pen, which I thought was a funny choice for an angry note. I used to love those glitter pens when I was in middle school. We'd write notes to each other and fold them into little triangles. I had this one friend, Sarah, who could fold them so perfectly they looked like origami. She ended up moving to Seattle in the eighth grade because her dad got a job at Boeing. I always wondered if it rains as much there as people say. But um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the pacing. No, not really. Not even a little bit. I mean, I'm basically here because I just don't care about anything anymore. Not my art classes, not the board-game nights I used to go to, not even hiking or trying to get back on my soccer team. I used to play midfield, mostly. The delivery guy who dropped it off was always wearing shorts, even in the middle of winter when it was snowing. I never understood how people can do that. It makes me cold just looking at them. I bought this huge, fluffy winter coat last year on sale, the kind that makes you look like a marshmallow, but it's so warm. It has these deep pockets that are perfect for keeping your hands warm if you forget your gloves. I lose gloves all the time. I'll take one off to answer my phone and then just leave it on a bench or something. But yeah... I just sit in my room now, and it feels like everything is just slipping away. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because... I just don’t care about anything anymore. It’s like all the things I used to do, I just can't be bothered. Not my art classes, or board-game nights, or hiking... I was even supposed to be getting back to the soccer team, but... I don't know. Now I just sit in my room while everything just kind of slips away. \n\nWhich is a shame because I used to really love those game nights. My friend Jessica used to host them on Thursdays, and she always made this amazing cheese dip in this tiny little slow cooker she found at a garage sale. It had these painted orange daisies on the side of it. She collects a lot of vintage kitchen stuff, actually, her whole apartment looks like it's stuck in the 1970s. She even has one of those rotary phones on the wall, though I don't think it actually works. I keep meaning to ask her where she finds all that stuff, but... yeah. I just stay in my room now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah... most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. It just sits there. It feels like there’s no future for me beyond the apartment walls. I stare at those walls a lot, actually. They have this weird textured wallpaper that looks kind of like oatmeal. I tried to peel a little piece off in the corner by the baseboard once, but it’s glued on there really tight. The landlord said it was installed in the nineties by the previous owners, who apparently had a bunch of cats. I’m actually allergic to cats, which is why I never got one, even though Jessica keeps telling me a pet would be good for me. She has a pug named Barnaby who snores louder than a person. He has this little plaid sweater he wears in the winter when she takes him for walks... but anyway, yeah. Just that heavy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy the things you used to love, even if you don't feel like doing them?\n\nPatient: No, I... I really don't. Like I was saying before, I just don't care about any of it anymore. The art classes, the hiking, the game nights... it's all just... nothing to me now. I don't even care about the soccer team, which is wild because I played for three years. We had these bright blue jerseys that always got grass stains on the elbows no matter how much you bleached them. I tried soaking mine in this special eco-friendly detergent once, but it just made it smell like pine needles. My mom used to use pine cleaner on our kitchen floors when I was a kid. She'd mop every Saturday morning while listening to ABBA on this old cassette player. The tape deck would always jam on \"Dancing Queen,\" and she'd have to pull the tape out with a pencil... but no, I don't enjoy anything. I just sit in my room and watch it all slip away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any pleasure at all when you do manage to engage in these activities?\n\nPatient: No, there's no pleasure. Like I said, I just don't care about anything anymore. It's not just that I don't feel like doing them, it's that when I try, or even think about trying, it's all just... flat. The art classes, the game nights, the hiking... even the soccer team. I just sit in my room while everything slips away. \n\nI used to have my art supplies set up by the window in my room. It's a nice window, actually, it gets the morning sun. But the glass has this tiny crack in the bottom corner. The landlord said a bird flew into it before I moved in. I always wondered what kind of bird it was. Maybe a robin? We used to have robins in our yard when I was little. They'd pull worms right out of the grass after it rained. The smell of rain on pavement always reminds me of that house. We had a driveway that was so steep, my dad's car used to scrape the bottom every time he pulled in... but yeah. No pleasure. I just sit in my room.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It's been a long time. I think it became constant around the following summer, so... maybe a year and four months now? That summer was really hot. I remember the air conditioner in my apartment broke right in the middle of July. The repair guy took three days to come out, and he was this older man who kept complaining about his knees. He said he needed surgery but couldn't afford the time off work. He had this big toolbox that was covered in stickers from different national parks. I've never been to a national park, actually. I always wanted to go to Yosemite. I saw a documentary about it once on public television. The narrator had this incredibly soothing voice, kind of like Morgan Freeman, but not him. I think I fell asleep watching it, which is weird because I usually have so much trouble sleeping. But yeah, about a year and four months of just... feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or speaking more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Well, it really depends on the day. Sometimes making coffee feels like I'm lifting wet cement. Every movement is just... dragging. The coffee maker I have is this old drip machine I bought at a garage sale. It's an awful shade of yellow, like mustard that's been left out in the sun too long. The lady who sold it to me had these huge plastic earrings shaped like parrots. They clacked against her jaw when she talked. She told me she was moving to Florida to live with her sister who breeds miniature poodles. I always thought poodles were kind of high-maintenance dogs, with all the grooming they need. My neighbor used to have a standard poodle that would bark at the mailman every single day right at 11:00 AM. Our mailman was this really tall guy who always wore shorts, even in December when it was snowing... but anyway. Yeah, sometimes I'm just so slow. But then other days, I just pace. I'll walk from the kitchen to the window over and over until my legs actually ache.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I've lost weight. My jeans have gotten so loose lately that I actually have to knot the waistband just to keep them up. I just... I go most of the day without eating because food just tastes like cardboard to me now. Even my favorite foods. I used to make this really good baked ziti. My grandmother taught me how to make the sauce from scratch. She had this enormous cast-iron pot that she brought over from Italy, and it weighed an absolute ton. I don't know how she lifted it, honestly, because she was only about four foot eleven. She used to wear these thick woolen sweaters, even in the summer, because she said her blood was thin. She kept peppermint candies in the pockets, the kind with the red and white swirls wrapped in that clear cellophane that crinkles really loudly. I tried to find those exact candies at the grocery store last week, but the aisle was being restocked by this teenager who was listening to music so loud I could hear the bass through his headphones. It was some kind of electronic dance music, I think. But anyway, yeah, I don't really eat much anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself pacing back and forth in your room?\n\nPatient: Well, I think I mentioned this just a bit ago, but yeah, I do. It's either one extreme or the other, honestly. Some days I'll just pace, like, back and forth from the kitchen to the window, over and over until my legs actually ache. The floorboards by the window squeak a little. It's an older building, you know? The landlord said it was built in the 1920s, back when they used real hardwood. He's this guy who always wears these faded polo shirts with the collars popped up, like he's stuck in an 80s movie or something. He drives this bright red convertible that he parks right in front of the building, taking up two spaces. My neighbor, the one who used to have the poodle, left a really angry note on his windshield once. She wrote it in pink glitter pen, which I thought was a funny choice for an angry note. I used to love those glitter pens when I was in middle school. We'd write notes to each other and fold them into little triangles. I had this one friend, Sarah, who could fold them so perfectly they looked like origami. She ended up moving to Seattle in the eighth grade because her dad got a job at Boeing. I always wondered if it rains as much there as people say. But um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the pacing. Yeah, I pace a lot on the days I'm not moving so slowly that, like I said, making coffee feels like I'm trying to lift wet cement.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I have. It's just... I keep having these thoughts that I just don't want to wake up anymore. It's like this heavy blanket over everything. My friend actually came over the other day and found me just sitting there, and I guess I had been saying out loud that I couldn't keep living like this. She got really scared. She's known me since we were in college. We took this art history class together, and the professor was this eccentric guy who always wore bow ties. He had a different bow tie for every day of the week, and on Fridays he'd wear one with little cartoon dinosaurs on it. He said his grandson picked it out for him. His grandson was named Oliver, I think, or maybe Leo. It was one of those names that got really popular a few years ago. I remember reading an article about how baby names go in cycles, like how all the vintage names from the 1920s are coming back now. My great-aunt was named Pearl, and she collected ceramic thimbles from all over the world. She had a whole display case for them in her hallway, right next to this grandfather clock that chimed so loudly it would make you jump if you weren't expecting it. But yeah, my friend found me and I was just saying I couldn't do it anymore. I just want to go to sleep and not wake up.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel like you're moving slowly or is it more that some days you're slow and others you're pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: I think I kind of just said this, but yeah, it really depends on the day. It's like I'm stuck between two extremes. Some days I just pace from the kitchen to the window, over and over again, until my legs literally ache. The linoleum in the kitchen is starting to peel up at the corners, actually. It's this awful mustard yellow color that I think was probably really trendy in the seventies. My mom had a kitchen that exact same color when I was growing up, complete with the matching appliances. Yeah, I do. It's... it's a woman's voice. She's always in the corner of my room, just telling me that I ruined everything and that I should just stay inside. And it's not like a thought, you know? The linoleum in the kitchen is starting to peel up at the corners, actually. It's this awful mustard yellow color that I think was probably really trendy in the seventies. My mom had a kitchen that exact same color when I was growing up, complete with the matching appliances. She had this blender that was so loud you couldn't hear yourself think when she made smoothies. She used to put wheat germ in them, which gave them this really gritty texture. I could never drink them, but my brother loved them. He lives in Denver now, working as a graphic designer. He designed the logo for this local brewery out there, the one with the mountain goat on it. I've never been to Denver, but I hear the altitude can really mess with you if you're not used to it. It's just exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Is the pacing or the slow movement more frequent for you overall?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it's hard to say which one happens more. I was trying to find a belt the other day, and I dug through my closet and found this old braided leather one I bought at a flea market years ago. It was one of those huge outdoor markets where people sell everything from antique furniture to homemade jam. I bought a jar of strawberry rhubarb jam from this older woman who was wearing a bright purple sun hat. She told me the rhubarb came from her own garden. She had this massive garden, she said, with tomatoes and zucchini and these huge sunflowers that grew taller than her fence. I tried to grow sunflowers once, but the squirrels kept digging up the seeds before they could even sprout. Squirrels are so persistent, you know? They just keep coming back. The delivery guy who dropped it off was always wearing shorts, even in the middle of winter when it was snowing. I never understood how people can do that. It makes me cold just looking at them. I bought this huge, fluffy winter coat last year on sale, the kind that makes you look like a marshmallow, but it's so warm. It has these deep pockets that are perfect for keeping your hands warm if you forget your gloves. I lose gloves all the time. I'll take one off to answer my phone and then just leave it on a bench or something. But yeah... I just sit in my room now, and it feels like everything is just slipping away. I don't even care enough to try and stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts or heard any voices that aren't your own?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's a woman's voice. She's always in the corner of my room, just telling me that I ruined everything and that I should just stay inside. And it's not like a thought, you know? I bought these blackout curtains to help with that, they're this deep navy blue color. I found them at a thrift store, and the lady at the register told me they originally came from a hotel that was being renovated downtown. That hotel used to have this incredible rooftop restaurant. I went there once for a friend's birthday party, and they served these little appetizers on silver trays. One of them was this goat cheese and fig tartlet, and I tried to recreate it at home once, but I burnt the pastry crust completely black. My smoke alarm went off and it took me twenty minutes to get it to stop beeping. I had to stand on a kitchen chair and wave a towel at it. But um, yeah, the voice is just... she's just always there, telling me to stay inside.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you experience any other unusual perceptions or hear voices when you're alone, aside from the woman's voice you mentioned?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Hallucinations\nmanifestation: \"I hear a woman’s voice from the corner of my room saying I ruined everything and should stay inside, and it sounds as clear as someone standing right next to my bed.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it's really just her. Just that one woman's voice. She's always over there in the corner, you know, just repeating that I ruined everything and that I need to stay inside. And like I said, it's so loud and clear, exactly like she's standing right next to my bed. I actually got a new comforter for that bed a few months ago because the old one was starting to get these weird little pills all over the fabric. It was supposed to be 100% cotton, but I think it was a blend because it got so scratchy. I ordered the new one online, and the delivery driver left it on my porch while it was raining. The box got completely soaked, but luckily the comforter was wrapped in plastic inside. It's this really nice sage green color, which is supposed to be calming, right? I read somewhere that green is the most restful color for the human eye. My aunt used to have her entire kitchen painted sage green, even the cabinets. She collected these vintage ceramic roosters and had them lined up all along the tops of the cabinets. I always thought they were going to fall off if someone slammed a door too hard. But yeah, no other voices or anything like that. Just her, always telling me to stay inside.

They clacked against her jaw when she talked. She told me she was moving to Florida to live with her sister who breeds miniature poodles. I always thought poodles were kind of high-maintenance dogs, with all the grooming they need. My neighbor used to have a standard poodle that would bark at the mailman every single day right at 11:00 AM. Our mailman was this really tall guy who always wore shorts, even in December when it was snowing... but anyway. Yeah, sometimes I'm just so slow. But then other days, I just pace. I'll walk from the kitchen to the window over and over until my legs actually ache.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I've lost weight. My jeans have gotten so loose lately that I actually have to knot the waistband just to keep them up. I just... I go most of the day without eating because food just tastes like cardboard to me now. Even my favorite foods. I used to make this really good baked ziti. My grandmother taught me how to make the sauce from scratch. She used to wear these thick woolen sweaters, even in the summer, because she said her blood was thin. It was some kind of electronic dance music, I think. He drives this bright red convertible that he parks right in front of the building, taking up two spaces. I used to love those glitter pens when I was in middle school. We'd write notes to each other and fold them into little triangles. I had this one friend, Sarah, who could fold them so perfectly they looked like origami. She ended up moving to Seattle in the eighth grade because her dad got a job at Boeing. I always wondered if it rains as much there as people say. But um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the pacing. Yeah, I pace a lot on the days I'm not moving so slowly that, like I said, making coffee feels like I'm trying to lift wet cement.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I have. It's just... I keep having these thoughts that I just don't want to wake up anymore. It's like this heavy blanket over everything. She's known me since we were in college. We took this art history class together, and the professor was this eccentric guy who always wore bow ties. He had a different bow tie for every day of the week, and on Fridays he'd wear one with little cartoon dinosaurs on it. He said his grandson picked it out for him. His grandson was named Oliver, I think, or maybe Leo. It was one of those names that got really popular a few years ago. I remember reading an article about how baby names go in cycles, like how all the vintage names from the 1920s are coming back now. My great-aunt was named Pearl, and she collected ceramic thimbles from all over the world. She had a whole display case for them in her hallway, right next to this grandfather clock that chimed so loudly it would make you jump if you weren't expecting it. But yeah, my friend found me and I was just saying I couldn't do it anymore. I just want to go to sleep and not wake up.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel like you're moving slowly or is it more that some days you're slow and others you're pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: I think I kind of just said this, but yeah, it really depends on the day. It's like I'm stuck between two extremes. Some days I just pace from the kitchen to the window, over and over again, until my legs literally ache. The linoleum in the kitchen is starting to peel up at the corners, actually. It's this awful mustard yellow color that I think was probably really trendy in the seventies. My mom had a kitchen that exact same color when I was growing up, complete with the matching appliances. She had this blender that was so loud you couldn't hear yourself think when she made smoothies. She used to put wheat germ in them, which gave them this really gritty texture. I could never drink them, but my brother loved them. He lives in Denver now, working as a graphic designer. He designed the logo for this local brewery out there, the one with the mountain goat on it. I've never been to Denver, but I hear the altitude can really mess with you if you're not used to it. But anyway, yeah, other times, I move so slowly that just trying to make a cup of coffee feels like I'm trying to lift wet cement. It's just exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Is the pacing or the slow movement more frequent for you overall?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it's hard to say which one happens more. I feel like I'm just always bouncing between them. Like I was saying, some days I'm just pacing, kitchen to the window, over and over until my legs are just throbbing. And then other days, I'm so slow, like trying to make coffee is like lifting wet cement. I actually bought this new coffee a few weeks ago, a dark roast from that little shop on the corner. The barista there has this really intricate tattoo of a compass on his forearm. He told me it took three sessions to finish. I've always thought about getting a tattoo, maybe a small bird or something on my ankle, but I'm terrified of needles. When I was a kid, I had to get blood drawn, and I actually passed out right there in the chair. The nurse had to give me a juice box and a sticker, like I was five years old. It was one of those scratch-and-sniff stickers, grape scented. I used to collect those in a binder. I had pages and pages of them. But um, yeah, the pacing and the slowness... they just kind of take turns ruining my week. I don't really keep track of which one wins.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight, such as more than 5% of your body weight, over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. I mean, I go most of the day without eating anything at all because everything just tastes like cardboard lately. It's like there's no flavor left in the world. And my jeans have gotten so loose I actually have to knot the waistband to keep them up. I was trying to find a belt the other day, and I dug through my closet and found this old braided leather one I bought at a flea market years ago. It was one of those huge outdoor markets where people sell everything from antique furniture to homemade jam. I bought a jar of strawberry rhubarb jam from this older woman who was wearing a bright purple sun hat. She told me the rhubarb came from her own garden. She had this massive garden, she said, with tomatoes and zucchini and these huge sunflowers that grew taller than her fence. I tried to grow sunflowers once, but the squirrels kept digging up the seeds before they could even sprout. Squirrels are so persistent, you know? They just keep coming back. But yeah, the weight thing... I just don't have any interest in food right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past month where you felt somewhat interested in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Not even a little bit. I mean, I'm basically here because I just don't care about anything anymore. Not my art classes, not the board-game nights I used to go to, not even hiking or trying to get back on my soccer team. I used to play midfield, mostly. Our jerseys were this really bright neon green color. We ordered them online from this custom sports apparel place, and when they arrived, the box smelled strongly of vinegar for some reason. The delivery guy who dropped it off was always wearing shorts, even in the middle of winter when it was snowing. I never understood how people can do that. It makes me cold just looking at them. I bought this huge, fluffy winter coat last year on sale, the kind that makes you look like a marshmallow, but it's so warm. It has these deep pockets that are perfect for keeping your hands warm if you forget your gloves. I lose gloves all the time. I'll take one off to answer my phone and then just leave it on a bench or something. But yeah... I just sit in my room now, and it feels like everything is just slipping away. I don't even care enough to try and stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts or heard any voices that aren't your own?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's a woman's voice. She's always in the corner of my room, just telling me that I ruined everything and that I should just stay inside. And it's not like a thought, you know? It sounds as clear as if someone is standing right next to my bed. My bed actually used to be against the other wall, but I moved it last spring because the morning sun was hitting my face too early. I had this one friend, Sarah, who could fold them so perfectly they looked like origami. She ended up moving to Seattle in the eighth grade because her dad got a job at Boeing. I always wondered if it rains as much there as people say. But um, what was I saying? Oh, right, the pacing. Yeah, I pace a lot on the days I'm not moving so slowly that, like I said, making coffee feels like I'm trying to lift wet cement.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I have. It's just... I keep having these thoughts that I just don't want to wake up anymore. It's like this heavy blanket over everything. My friend actually came over the other day and found me just sitting there, and I guess I had been saying out loud that I couldn't keep living like this. She got really scared. She's known me since we were in college. We took this art history class together, and the professor was this eccentric guy who always wore bow ties. He had a different bow tie for every day of the week, and on Fridays he'd wear one with little cartoon dinosaurs on it. He said his grandson picked it out for him. His grandson was named Oliver, I think, or maybe Leo. It was one of those names that got really popular a few years ago. I remember reading an article about how baby names go in cycles, like how all the vintage names from the 1920s are coming back now. My great-aunt was named Pearl, and she collected ceramic thimbles from all over the world. She had a whole display case for them in her hallway, right next to this grandfather clock that chimed so loudly it would make you jump if you weren't expecting it. But yeah, my friend found me and I was just saying I couldn't do it anymore. I just want to go to sleep and not wake up.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel like you're moving slowly or is it more that some days you're slow and others you're pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: I think I kind of just said this, but yeah, it really depends on the day. It's like I'm stuck between two extremes. Some days I just pace from the kitchen to the window, over and over again, until my legs literally ache. The linoleum in the kitchen is starting to peel up at the corners, actually. It's this awful mustard yellow color that I think was probably really trendy in the seventies. My mom had a kitchen that exact same color when I was growing up, complete with the matching appliances. She had this blender that was so loud you couldn't hear yourself think when she made smoothies. She used to put wheat germ in them, which gave them this really gritty texture. I could never drink them, but my brother loved them. He lives in Denver now, working as a graphic designer. He designed the logo for this local brewery out there, the one with the mountain goat on it. I've never been to Denver, but I hear the altitude can really mess with you if you're not used to it. But anyway, yeah, other times, I move so slowly that just trying to make a cup of coffee feels like I'm trying to lift wet cement. It's just exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Is the pacing or the slow movement more frequent for you overall?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it's hard to say which one happens more. I feel like I'm just always bouncing between them. Like I was saying, some days I'm just pacing, kitchen to the window, over and over until my legs are just throbbing. And then other days, I'm so slow, like trying to make coffee is like lifting wet cement. I actually bought this new coffee a few weeks ago, a dark roast from that little shop on the corner. The barista there has this really intricate tattoo of a compass on his forearm. He told me it took three sessions to finish. I've always thought about getting a tattoo, maybe a small bird or something on my ankle, but I'm terrified of needles. When I was a kid, I had to get blood drawn, and I actually passed out right there in the chair. The nurse had to give me a juice box and a sticker, like I was five years old. It was one of those scratch-and-sniff stickers, grape scented. I used to collect those in a binder. I had pages and pages of them. But um, yeah, the pacing and the slowness... they just kind of take turns ruining my week. I don't really keep track of which one wins.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight, such as more than 5% of your body weight, over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. I mean, I go most of the day without eating anything at all because everything just tastes like cardboard lately. It's like there's no flavor left in the world. And my jeans have gotten so loose I actually have to knot the waistband to keep them up. I was trying to find a belt the other day, and I dug through my closet and found this old braided leather one I bought at a flea market years ago. It was one of those huge outdoor markets where people sell everything from antique furniture to homemade jam. I bought a jar of strawberry rhubarb jam from this older woman who was wearing a bright purple sun hat. She told me the rhubarb came from her own garden. She had this massive garden, she said, with tomatoes and zucchini and these huge sunflowers that grew taller than her fence. I tried to grow sunflowers once, but the squirrels kept digging up the seeds before they could even sprout. Squirrels are so persistent, you know? They just keep coming back. Not my art classes, not the board-game nights I used to go to, not even hiking or trying to get back on my soccer team. I used to play midfield, mostly. Our jerseys were this really bright neon green color. We ordered them online from this custom sports apparel place, and when they arrived, the box smelled strongly of vinegar for some reason. The delivery guy who dropped it off was always wearing shorts, even in the middle of winter when it was snowing. I never understood how people can do that. It makes me cold just looking at them. I bought this huge, fluffy winter coat last year on sale, the kind that makes you look like a marshmallow, but it's so warm. It has these deep pockets that are perfect for keeping your hands warm if you forget your gloves. I lose gloves all the time. I'll take one off to answer my phone and then just leave it on a bench or something. But yeah... I just sit in my room now, and it feels like everything is just slipping away. I don't even care enough to try and stop it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts or heard any voices that aren't your own?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... it's a woman's voice. She's always in the corner of my room, just telling me that I ruined everything and that I should just stay inside. And it's not like a thought, you know? It sounds as clear as if someone is standing right next to my bed. My bed actually used to be against the other wall, but I moved it last spring because the morning sun was hitting my face too early. I bought these blackout curtains to help with that, they're this deep navy blue color. I found them at a thrift store, and the lady at the register told me they originally came from a hotel that was being renovated downtown. That hotel used to have this incredible rooftop restaurant. I went there once for a friend's birthday party, and they served these little appetizers on silver trays. One of them was this goat cheese and fig tartlet, and I tried to recreate it at home once, but I burnt the pastry crust completely black. My smoke alarm went off and it took me twenty minutes to get it to stop beeping. I had to stand on a kitchen chair and wave a towel at it. But um, yeah, the voice is just... she's just always there, telling me to stay inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any other unusual perceptions or hear voices when you're alone, aside from the woman's voice you mentioned?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just her. Just that one woman's voice. She's always over there in the corner, you know, just repeating that I ruined everything and that I need to stay inside. And like I said, it's so loud and clear, exactly like she's standing right next to my bed. The box got completely soaked, but luckily the comforter was wrapped in plastic inside. It's this really nice sage green color, which is supposed to be calming, right?
